Treat complications according to their respective humoral causes.
Thus ends the thirty-third chapter (त्रयस्त्रिंशः अध्यायः/thirty-third chapter) named 'Ocular Pooling and Cooked Extract Therapies' (तर्पणपुटपाकविधिः/ocular pooling and cooked extract rules) of Ashtanga Sangraha.




Now, therefore, we shall explain the chapter (अध्याय/section) on surgical blunt instruments and sharp instruments (यन्त्रशस्त्र विधि/blunt and sharp tools) of Ashtanga Sangraha.
Foreign bodies (शल्य/foreign bodies) are harmful entities that inflict pain and injury upon both mind and body.
Surgical blunt instruments (यन्त्र/blunt instruments) are the mechanical means used for extracting diverse foreign bodies lodged in various anatomical regions, for applying sharp scalpels (शस्त्र/scalpel), caustic alkali (क्षार/caustic alkali), and cautery (अग्नि/cautery) in hemorrhoids (अर्शस्/piles) and fistula-in-ano (भगन्दर/fistula), and for protecting healthy surrounding body parts.
Similarly, animal horns (शृङ्ग/animal horn), gourds (अलाबु/gourd), and suction pots (घटिका/suction pot) are used for suction, while iron probes and Jambavashtha (जाम्बवौष्ठ/blackplum applicators) are used for cautery and enema application.
Numerous other instruments of various shapes and functions are employed as surgical appliances for healthy and diseased individuals.
Hence, because their forms depend on specific surgical procedures, it is impossible to fix an absolute limit on their total count.
However, ancient surgical authorities state that there are conventionally one hundred and one blunt instruments (एकविंशति शतोत्तर यन्त्र/101 blunt instruments).
In this treatise, they are broadly classified into six fundamental categories (षड्विध यन्त्र/six instrument classes).
Namely: cruciform forceps (स्वस्तिक/cruciform forceps), pincer forceps (सन्दंश/pincer forceps), palatal scoops (ताल/palatal scoops), tubular speculums (नाडी/tubular speculums), rod-like probes (शलाका/rod-like probes), and auxiliary accessories (अनुयन्त्र/auxiliary tools).
Cruciform forceps (स्वस्तिक यन्त्र/cruciform forceps) are named after the distinct animal and bird heads they resemble, such as herons, lions, tigers, vultures, hawks, and crocodiles.
They are generally constructed of forged iron (लौह/iron) and measure eighteen fingers (अष्टादशाङ्गुल/18 fingers) in length.
Their two arms are joined at the center by a lentil-shaped rivet pin (कील/rivet pin), and their handle ends are curved inward like elephant goads (अङ्कुश/elephant goad) for extracting foreign bodies lodged in bones.
Forceps resembling heads of lions, tigers, serpents, and crocodiles should be used for extracting prominent, visible foreign bodies.
Forceps resembling heads of herons, crows, and hawks should be selected according to the specific shape of the wound for deeper foreign bodies.
Pincer forceps (सन्दंश यन्त्र/pincer forceps) are of two types: with a catch ring (सनिबन्धन/with catch) and without a catch ring (निर्निबन्धन/without catch), measuring sixteen fingers in length.
They are indicated for extracting foreign bodies lodged in skin (त्वच्/skin), flesh (मांस/muscle tissue), blood vessels (सिरा/vein), and ligaments (स्नायु/ligament).
Another small pincer forceps measures six fingers in length and two fingers in width, with curved arms forming a flat meeting tip like thumb and index finger, used for extracting fine foreign bodies, eyelashes, and stomatitis tumors.
Similarly, Muchundi forceps (मुचुण्डी/fine-toothed forceps) is straight, smooth, fine-toothed, two-armed, bound at base with a ring, and compresses tissue to remove pterygium remnants and deep-seated ulcer tumors.
Palatal scoops (ताल यन्त्र/palatal scoops) are two in number, each measuring twelve fingers in length.
Shaped like a single fish gill or double fish gills (मत्स्यगल/fish gill), they are used for extracting foreign bodies lodged in ear canals and nasal passages.
Tubular speculums (नाडी यन्त्र/tubular speculums) are hollow instruments of diverse types, shapes, and applications, having apertures at one or both ends.
They are used for inspecting and extracting foreign bodies in deep body channels (स्रोतः/body channel), inspecting lesions, facilitating surgical procedures, and suction.
Their circumference should match the diameter of the body channel entrance, and their length should be customized according to procedure.
For inspecting foreign bodies in the throat, a tubular speculum should measure ten fingers in length and five fingers in circumference.
For holding two-eared foreign bodies, a tube with three inspection apertures matching the foreign body size should be constructed.
Similarly, for four-eared foreign bodies, a tube with five inspection apertures should be constructed.
The foreign body extractor tube (शल्यनिर्घातनी/foreign body extractor) has a head shaped like a lotus calyx, measuring twelve fingers in length with a three-finger hollow bore.
Proctoscopes for hemorrhoids (अर्शोयन्त्र/proctoscope speculum) are of three types.
Constructed of iron, ivory, horn, or wood, shaped like a cow's nipple, four fingers long, measuring five fingers in circumference for men,
And six fingers in circumference for women.
One type has two slots for inspection; the second type has one slot for performing surgical, caustic, or cautery operations.
Detailed construction follows:
The side slot allows clear inspection and precise application of scalpel or caustic alkali without overshooting; the slot measures three fingers in length and thumb-belly width.
At the remaining un-slotted portion, an elevated circular collar half a finger high is constructed above and below the slot.
The third type, named Shamyakhya (शाम्याख्य/solid proctoscope), is identical in shape but lacks side slots, used solely for compressing hemorrhoidal masses.
In fistula-in-ano speculums, the upper lip border above the side slot should be removed.
Similarly, nasal polyp speculums (घ्राणार्शो यन्त्र/nasal speculum) are tubular, two fingers long, single-slotted, with circumference matching index finger width.
Finger guards (अङ्गुलित्राणक/finger guard) fit over fingers during mouth procedures, thick-lipped, four fingers long, cow-nipple shaped, with top and bottom holes, made of ivory, horn, or wood.
It is bound securely to the wrist with a strong cord during oral surgical evacuations.
Vaginal/Uterine speculum (योनिव्रण यन्त्र/vaginal speculum) measures sixteen fingers in length, hollow inside, with four expanding wall rods forming a lotus bud tip that expands upon pressing the base rods.
Sinus washing speculums (नाडीव्रण यन्त्र/sinus irrigation tube) measure six fingers in length, shaped like enema nozzles without side collars, with thumb and pea-sized apertures at base and tip.
In abdominal dropsy (बकोदर/ascites), a double-ended hollow tube or a quill tube (पिच्छ नाडी/quill tube) should be inserted for draining fluid.
Specialized tubes for oil enema, urethral enema, powder blowing, medicated smoke, and hydrocele drainage are five in number, constructed according to their specific requirements.
Suction horn (शृङ्ग/suction horn) is short, medium, or long (18 or 12 fingers long), three fingers wide at base, with a mustard-seed aperture at tip covered with thin leather like a nipple.
It is used for extracting trapped Vata, poison, vitiated blood, serum, and corrupted breastmilk.
For sucking Kapha-corrupted blood, a hollow gourd (अलाबु/gourd) is used.
It measures twelve fingers in length, eighteen fingers in circumference, three to four fingers wide at mouth, containing ignited cotton cloth inside to create suction.
Similarly, an earthen suction pot (घटी/earthen pot) is applied using heat and suction.
It is specifically used for elevating and dissolving abdominal tumors (गुल्म/phantom tumor).
Rod-like probes (शलाका यन्त्र/rod probes) are of diverse shapes, functions, lengths, and circumferences.
Among them, two probes shaped like earthworm heads are used for probing sinus tracts.
Two probes measuring eight and nine fingers in length with lentil-seed shaped tips are used for extracting foreign bodies from channels.
There are six types of hooks or pegs (शङ्कु/retractor hooks).
Two cobra-hood probes measuring twelve and sixteen fingers in length are used for tissue retraction.
Two arrow-feather probes measuring ten and twelve fingers in length are used for dislodging foreign bodies.
Two fish-hook tipped probes are used for extracting foreign bodies.
An obstetric hook (गर्भशङ्कु/obstetric hook) resembles a peg, measures eight fingers in length with a bent tip, used for extracting obstructed fetuses (मूढगर्भ/obstructed fetus).
A serpent-hood probe is used for dislodging trapped stones.
Two cotton-swab probes (कर्पासचोल/cotton-swab probe) measuring ten and twelve fingers in length are used for wiping pus from sinus tracts and ulcers.
Three cautery and alkali probes (अग्निक्षार शलाका/cautery probes) are shaped like a spoon, a jujube berry, and a rod for applying thermal cautery and caustic alkali.
A spoon-headed probe with a small bowl is used for cauterizing hemorrhoids.
A tea-spoon probe (कोलमात्र मुख/teaspoon probe) is used for applying caustic alkali to nasal polyps.
Collyrium applicator probes (अञ्जन शलाका/collyrium rod) measure ten fingers in length, thin in middle with pea-shaped bud tips at both ends.
Urethral catheters (मूत्रमार्ग नाडी/urethral catheter) are smooth, hollow rods matching male/female urethral dimensions for relieving urinary retention.
Accessory auxiliary instruments (अनुयन्त्र/auxiliary tools) include leather straps, silk threads, bandages, magnet (अयस्कान्त/magnet), palm leaves, caustic alkali, fire, leeches, and fingernails.
These auxiliary tools are employed for extraction, compression, binding, and protection.
Now, twenty sharp surgical instruments (विंशति शस्त्र/twenty sharp instruments) will be detailed.
Namely: Mandalagra (मंडलाग्र/circular scalpel), Vriddhipatra (वृद्धिपत्र/razor scalpel), Utpalapatra (उत्पलपत्र/lotus leaf scalpel), Ardhadhara (अर्धधार/single-edge scalpel), Mudrika (मुद्रिका/ring knife), Mudrikashastra (मुद्रिकाशस्त्र/ring scalpel), Kartari (कर्तरी/surgical scissors), Sarpamukha (सर्पमुख/serpent-mouth scalpel), Karapatra (करपत्र/bone saw), Kushapatra (कुशपत्र/kusha leaf blade), Atamukha (आटामुख/bird-beak blade), Sharapunkhamukha (शरपुङ्खमुख/arrow-feather blade), Antarmukha (अंतर्मुख/crescent internal blade), Kutharika (कुठारिका/small axe lancet), Vrihimukha (व्रीहिमुख/paddy-grain needle), Shalaka (शलाका/couching needle), Ara (आरा/awl punch), Karナビhyadhana (कर्णव्यधन/ear piercer), Suchi (सूची/surgical needle), and Kusha (कुश/needle brush).
Mandalagra scalpel has a circular tip expanded inside like an index finger, used for scraping (लेखन/scraping) and incising (छेदन/excision) in eyelid diseases, palate tumors, and stomatitis.
Vriddhipatra scalpel resembles a razor, curved or straight with a pointed needle tip, used for incising elevated or deep abscesses (विद्रधि/abscess).
Utpalapatra scalpel resembles a lotus leaf blade, sharp-pointed, used for incising and puncturing fleshy abscesses.
Ardhadhara scalpel has a single cutting edge, used for fine tissue dissections.
Mudrikashastra (ring scalpel) has a half-finger blade mounted on a finger ring worn on the index finger, bound with thread, used for throat operations.
Kartari (surgical scissors) has two arms joined at center with loop handles, used for cutting ligaments (स्नायु/ligaments), hair, and sutures.
Sarpamukha scalpel has a curved serpent-mouth tip with a half-finger blade, used for excising nasal polyps and ear tumors.
Karapatra (bone saw) measures ten fingers in length, two fingers in width, with fine teeth and rough cutting edge, used for sawing bones (अस्थि/bone).
Kushapatra and Atamukha scalpels have two-finger cutting blades, used for making straight surgical incisions.
Antarmukha scalpel has an internal crescent-shaped blade measuring one and a half fingers, used for internal incisions.
Kutharika (small axe lancet) has a thick handle shaped like a cow's tooth with a half-finger blade, used for puncturing blood vessels over bones.
Vrihimukha scalpel has a paddy-grain shaped blade measuring one and a half fingers, used for venesection (सिराव्यध/venesection) in fleshy areas, hydrocele, abdominal paracentesis, and abscesses.
Shalaka couching needle is double-ended with kuravaka bud tips, made of copper, used for couching cataract opacities (लिङ्गनाश/cataract).
Ara (awl punch) is four-angled with a half-finger round tip, used for puncturing thick earlobes and deep abscesses.
Ear piercer (कर्णव्यधन/ear piercer) measures three fingers in length with a jasmine-bud tip for piercing earlobes.
Three surgical needles (सूची/surgical needle) are round with strong eyes: a 3-finger triangular needle for fleshy parts; a 2-finger round needle for joints/bones; and a 1.5-finger curved bow-shaped needle for internal organs.
Needle brush (सूचीकूर्च/needle brush) has seven or eight needles bound on a single handle, used for scraping skin diseases (कुष्ठ/psoriasis), vitiligo (श्वित्र/vitiligo), hyperpigmentation (व्यङ्ग/hyperpigmentation), and alopecia (इन्द्रलुप्त/alopecia).
Khaja (scraper rod) has an eight-thorn copper/iron tip measuring half a finger, used for scraping internal nasal hemorrhage.
Two probing needles (एषणी/probing needle): one 8-finger smooth probe for searching sinus tracts; another needle-shaped probe tied with caustic thread (क्षारसूत्र/caustic thread) for cutting fistula-in-ano.
Badisha (sharp hook) has a deeply bent needle-sharp tip, used for grasping uvula enlargement (गलशुण्डिका/uvulitis) and pterygium.
Nakhashastra (nail-clipper knife) measures eight fingers in length with horse-ear or calf-tooth tips for extracting fine foreign bodies.
Auxiliary sharp tools (अनुशस्त्र/auxiliary tools) include leeches (जलौका/leeches), caustic alkali (क्षार/caustic alkali), cautery (अग्नि/cautery), glass, crystal, fingernails, rough leaves, cuttlefish bone, and dry cow dung.
The surgeon should dynamically design and adapt instruments according to individual surgical requirements.
Among all instruments, the surgeon's hand (हस्त/surgeon's hand) is supreme, because all instruments depend entirely on hand control.
Eight defects of blunt instruments (यन्त्र दोष/blunt tool defects): too long, too short, too thick, bent, thin, uneven grip, slipping, and loose joint.
Eight defects of sharp scalpels (शस्त्र दोष/scalpel defects): blunt edge, notched edge, rough edge, thick edge, thin edge, bent, long, or short.
Tempering sharp instruments (शस्त्र पायन/blade tempering): alkali tempering for cutting bone and arrows; water tempering for cutting flesh; oil tempering for venesection and sinus incision.
Cutting edges: demon-edge (आसुरी/sharp edge) for excision; half-demon edge for scraping; wheat-edge (कौशिकी/fine edge) for venesection.
Grasping scalpels: hold excision and scraping scalpels at junction of blade and handle with thumb, index, and middle fingers.
Hold bloodletting scalpels at tip of handle.
Hold paddy-grain scalpels with index finger and thumb, concealing handle in palm.
Hold extraction forceps at base.
Hold pincers and scissors above catch ring.
Sharpening stone (शिलिका/sharpening stone) should be a smooth stone matching blackgram color.
Blade protective board (शाल्मली फलक/blade board) made of silk-cotton wood.
Even a well-read surgeon must not perform independent operations without extensive practical simulation training (योग्या/practical simulation), to avoid injuring veins, ligaments, and vital organs.
Simulation training: incision on gourds and watermelons; excision on leather bags filled with water/mud; scraping on hairy skin; probing on rotten wood/lotus stalks; venesection on lotus stalks or water-filled leather bladders; suturing on soft cloth/leather.
Enema squeezing simulation: practicing on a water-filled pot with a side tube.
Anatomical Dissection Protocol (शरीर विशसन/anatomical dissection): To gain direct structural knowledge, select a cadaver of a person with all body parts intact, not killed by poison, nor suffering from long chronic illness.
Remove gastrointestinal contents, wrap the body in munj/balvaja grass, enclose it in a protective cage, and immerse it in a slow-flowing river in a hidden spot for seven days to decompose.
When thoroughly decomposed, remove the cadaver, scrape skin and tissues layer by layer using a brush of vetiver or bamboo fibers, and examine all external and internal organs, blood vessels, nerves, ligaments, and joints with direct vision.
Direct visual inspection (प्रत्यक्ष/direct vision) combined with scriptural knowledge (शास्त्र/scripture) grants complete surgical mastery.
Scalpel Sheath (शस्त्रकोश/scalpel sheath): measures nine fingers in width and twelve fingers in length, made of silk, linen, wool, or soft leather, well-stitched with internal pockets and secured with a rod closure.
Thus ends the thirty-fourth chapter (चतुस्त्रिंशः अध्यायः/thirty-fourth chapter) named 'Surgical Instruments and Metallic Tools' (यन्त्रशस्त्रविधिः/surgical instruments) of Ashtanga Sangraha.
For wounds in joints, bones, or areas with sparse muscle, use a 2-finger round needle.
For vital spots in stomach or intestines, use a 1.5-finger curved bow-shaped needle with paddy-grain tip.
Multi-needle brush (सूचीकूर्च/needle brush) with 7-8 needles on a single handle for leprosy, leucoderma, hyperpigmentation, and alopecia.
Khaja scraper rod (खज/scraper rod), 0.5 finger long with 8 thorn-tips in copper or iron, for nasal internal bloodletting.
Two smooth probes (एषणी/probing rod).
One probe (8 fingers long) for exploring sinus tracks, sinus wounds, and deep sinuses.
The other probe is needle-shaped, tied with alkali-soaked thread (क्षारसूत्र/alkali thread) for cutting sinus tracts and fistula-in-ano.
Curved sharp-tipped hook (बडिश/sharp hook) for grasping uvula and tonsils.
Nail scalpel (नखशस्त्र/nail knife), 8 fingers long, with horse-ear or calf-tooth blade for removing fine foreign bodies.
Auxiliary surgical tools (अनुशस्त्राणि/auxiliary tools) include leeches, alkali, cautery, sun-crystals, glass, quartz, iron-slag, fingernails, rough leaves, cuttlefish bone, and dried cowdung.
Using clinical judgment, the surgeon should select appropriate blunt and sharp instruments for surgical procedures.
The surgeon's hand (हस्त/surgeon's hand) is the supreme instrument, as all tools depend entirely upon it.
Eight blunt instrument defects: too long, too short, too thick, curved, too thin, crooked, difficult to grip, or loose joints.
Eight sharp scalpel defects: first 5 defects plus bluntness, chipped edge, or rough jagged edge (except saws).
Bone saws are excluded from the smooth edge defect rule.
Scalpels tempered in alkali solution are used for cutting arrows/bones; in water for cutting flesh; in oil for venesection and ligament cutting.
Edge sharpness: razor-sharp for cutting/excision; half-razor sharp for scraping; hair-thin for puncturing/draining.
Hold cutting, slitting, and scraping scalpels firmly at the junction of handle and blade with index finger, middle finger, and thumb.
Hold draining scalpels at the tip of the handle.
Hold paddy-grain lancet by thumb and index finger with blade tip hidden in palm for oral procedures.
Hold foreign body extraction tools at the handle base.
Hold pincers and scissors above the joint loop.
Hold remaining tools appropriately to ensure procedural ease and precision.
The sharpening stone (निशानी/sharpening stone) for surgical instruments should be a smooth stone block with the dark luster of blackgram or greenmung.
The edge-protecting board (धारासंस्थापन/edge-protecting board) should be made from soft silk-cotton tree wood (शाल्मली/silk-cotton tree).
Even a physician well-versed in theoretical scriptures should not perform surgical operations without extensive practical simulation training, as inexperienced cutting risks fatal injury to veins, ligaments, and vital spots (मर्म/vital organ).
Therefore, the aspiring surgeon must practice incision, excision, scraping, and extraction on gourds, watermelons, hides, mud-filled water bladders, and lotus stalks according to specific surgical techniques.
Similarly, enema squeezing technique should be practiced using a water-filled pot with a side outlet tube.
Furthermore, regarding anatomical dissection:
An aspiring surgeon desirous of mastering anatomy should secure an intact human cadaver—free from poison or chronic debilitating illness—remove intestinal contents, wrap it in reed grass inside a cage, and place it in a dark current of a flowing river to decompose for seven days.
When thoroughly decomposed, the body should be retrieved, placed on a flat board, and gently scraped layer by layer using vetiver or bamboo brushes to observe all skin layers, muscles, blood vessels (सिरा/blood vessel), ligaments (स्नायु/ligament), joints, and internal organs with one's own eyes alongside scriptural guidance.
Direct visual observation (प्रत्यक्ष/direct visual perception) combined with scriptural knowledge (शास्त्र/scriptural science) doubles the physician's wisdom and diagnostic perfection.
Both scriptural theory and direct anatomical observation together illuminate complete medical knowledge.
The surgical instrument case (शस्त्रकोश/surgical instrument bag) should be 9 fingers wide and 12 fingers long.
It should be crafted from fine linen, wool, silk, or soft leather.
It should have well-stitched compartments, inner wool lining, and strong loops for securing each tool.
The surgical bag should be closed with a wooden or metallic pin and kept well-stocked.
Thus ends the thirty-fourth chapter (चतुस्त्रिंशः अध्यायः/thirty-fourth chapter) named 'Surgical Instruments and Metallic Tools' (यन्त्रशस्त्रविधिः/surgical instruments) of Ashtanga Sangraha.




Now, therefore, we shall explain the chapter (अध्याय/section) on leech therapy and bloodletting rules (जलौकोविधिः/leech therapy rules) of Ashtanga Sangraha.
For bloodletting (रक्तविस्त्रावण/bloodletting) in kings, wealthy persons, timid individuals, delicate people, children, elderly, and women, leeches (जलौका/leeches) should be applied.
They are broadly classified into two categories: poisonous leeches (सविष जलौका/poisonous leeches) and non-poisonous leeches (निर्विष जलौका/non-poisonous leeches).
Poisonous leeches originate in putrid water contaminated by dead bodies, rot, urine, and feces of poisonous snakes, frogs, and fish; they are blood-red, white, extremely black, unusually thin or thick, hyperactive, slimy, thick-waisted, hairy, or marked with colorful rainbow-like lines.
Their bite causes burning sensation (दाह/burning), swelling (शोफ/swelling), suppuration (पाक/suppuration), itching (कण्डू/itching), skin eruptions (पिटिका/eruptions), erysipelas (विसर्प/erysipelas), fever (ज्वर/fever), fainting (मूर्च्छा/fainting), and leukoderma (श्वित्र/vitiligo).
In such bites, therapies counteracting poison, Pitta humor (पित्त/pitta humor), and blood morbidity should be promptly performed.
Non-poisonous leeches originate in sweet, clean, vast ponds rich in lotus, lily, fragrant water lilies, and moss; they are moss-green, dark blue-striped, or bronze-colored.
The maximum length for all leeches is 18 fingers (अङ्गुल/finger length).
Those measuring 4, 5, or 6 fingers should be applied to human beings (नृ/humans).
Larger leeches are used for elephants (गज/elephant) and horses (वाजि/horse).
Delicate leeches with thin skin, small heads, and broad abdomens are considered female leeches (स्त्री/female leech).
Leeches with opposite features, crescent-shaped, and thick rounded front ends are male leeches (पुमान्/male leech).
In diseases with massive humoral morbidity or chronic longstanding duration, male leeches should be applied.
In mild, recent, or delicate conditions, female leeches should be applied.
Leeches should be caught using wet leather traps and kept in a fresh earthen pot filled with fragrant mud and clean water.
For their nourishment, sweet, cold, pure water powdered with water chestnut, tubers, lotus stalks, moss, soft clay, and lotus seeds should be supplied.
To prevent putrefaction from their saliva and secretions, their water and food should be replaced every 3 days.
Every 5 days, they must be transferred to a new fresh pot of similar setup.
Leeches faint or lose vitality if they fail to vomit sucked bad blood completely or if exposed to intense sun heat.
Lethargic, motionless leeches in water that refuse food should be identified and discarded.
Healthy leeches should be placed in turmeric-mustard paste water or sour gruel to purge impurities, revived in pure water, and then applied to the target site after smearing the patient's skin with fine clay or dried cowdung powder.
If leeches refuse to bite, apply a drop of milk (क्षीर/milk), ghee (घृत/ghee), butter, or fresh blood, or make a slight skin prick.
When they attach their mouth like a horse-hoof arch, raise their neck, and suck blood with rhythmic head throbbing like a nursing infant, cover them gently with a moist soft cloth.
Sprinkle tiny drops of cool water over them continuously.
Just as a swan separates milk from a mixture of milk and water,
Similarly, leeches selectively suck out the vitiated blood (दुष्ट रक्त/impure blood) first from morbid blood pools.
When the patient feels pricking pain (तोद/pricking pain) or itching at the bite site, it signifies pure blood is being reached; remove the leech immediately to protect pure blood.
If a greedy leech refuses to detach, sprinkle honey or rock salt powder (लवण/rock salt) on its mouth.
Once fallen, smear its body with rice bran, apply oil-salt to its mouth, and gently squeeze it from tail to mouth to ensure complete emesis (वमन/emesis) of sucked blood.
Then place it back in fresh water as before.
A leech should not be applied again for seven full days (सप्तरात्र/seven days).
If residual bad blood remains at the bite site, gently rub with honey or jaggery to allow further oozing.
Once sufficient blood is shed, wash the bite with cold water and cover with a ghee-soaked cotton pad.
If bleeding persists or tissue remains inflamed, apply a cooling, sweet, astringent herbal paste mixed with ghee to soothe the site.
Proper, inadequate, or excessive bloodletting symptoms and management should be observed just like in venesection.
With the departure of morbid blood, swelling subsides (श्वयथु शान्ति/subsidence of swelling), and burning, redness, and pain are relieved.
Blood vitiated by Pitta humor should not be drawn with gourd or pot cups due to heat/fire contact; but blood vitiated by Kapha should be drawn using them.
Blood vitiated by Kapha should not be drawn using animal horns due to clotting; but blood vitiated by Vata and Pitta should be drawn using horns.
After scarifying (प्रच्छान/scarification) the skin, suck blood using an animal horn whose tip is bound with thin cloth.
Or suck blood using gourd cups or earthen pots containing a burning cotton swab inside.
Here occurs a summary stanza:
Firmly bind the body part above the site evenly with a rope or cloth band,
And perform scarification (प्रच्छान/scarification) while strictly avoiding ligaments, joints, bones, and vital spots (मर्म/vital organ).
Make fine vertical incisions moving from below upward in parallel lines,
Neither too deep, dense, nor transverse, without overlapping previous incisions.
Localized morbid blood should be easily drawn using scarification followed by horns, gourds, or cups.
Clotted blood is drawn by leeches; widespread deep blood morbidity is drawn by venesection (सिराव्यध/venesection).
Scarification is used for localized lumps; leeches for deep-seated localized blood;
Gourds, pots, and horns for skin-level blood; venesection for generalized systemic blood morbidity.
Vata-vitiated blood is drawn by horn (शृङ्ग/horn); Pitta-vitiated blood by leech (जलौका/leech); Kapha-vitiated blood by gourd (अलाबु/gourd).
If Vata gets aggravated after bloodletting due to excessive cold pastes,
Causing pricking pain, itching, and swelling, sprinkle the site with warm medicated ghee.
Thus ends the thirty-fifth chapter (पञ्चत्रिंशः अध्यायः/thirty-fifth chapter) named 'Leech Therapy and Bloodletting Rules' (जलौकोविधिः/leech therapy rules) of Ashtanga Sangraha.




Now, therefore, we shall explain the chapter (अध्याय/section) on venesection and vein puncturing rules (सिराव्यध विधि/venesection rules) of Ashtanga Sangraha.
Though many methods of bloodletting (रक्तमोक्षण/bloodletting) have been mentioned earlier, venesection (सिराव्यध/vein puncturing) is paramount among all surgical and purificatory therapies (संशोधन/purification) for diseases curable by it.
Because by venesection, blood-borne diseases are uprooted from their origin, just as paddy crops perish when the irrigation canal bank is breached.
And so it is said:
Venesection (सिराव्यध/venesection) constitutes half or even the entirety of surgical treatment (शल्य चिकित्सा/surgical treatment), just as enema therapy (बस्ति/enema therapy) is considered half of internal medicine (कायचिकित्सा/internal medicine).
In major surgical tradition, venesection is recognized as the supreme operative measure.
Just as blood (रक्त/blood) is the primary seat and substratum of various systemic diseases,
When blood is the primary vitiated tissue (दूष्य/dushya), this vein-puncturing procedure is the first-line treatment to be applied.
In its natural state, blood (रक्त/blood) is derived from the essence of digested food called plasma chyme (रस/chyme), colored red by balanced internal fire heat (तेजस्/fire heat), resembling cochineal insects (इन्द्रगोप/red cochineal), rabbit blood, lotus blossoms, melted gold, or lac dye; when washed with water it clears without staining, tasting sweet and slightly salty, unctuous, neither hot nor cold, heavy, pacifying Pitta accumulation, possessing both cooling and thermal attributes (सौम्याग्नेय/cool and fiery nature); it is the fundamental tissue susceptible to morbidity.
Some ancient authorities classify blood as a humor (दोष/humor).
Others consider it possessing attributes of both humor and tissue (उभयात्मक/dual nature).
When kept pure by wholesome diet and daily routine, pure blood forms the root origin of physical strength (बल/strength), complexion (वर्ण/complexion), happiness, and longevity.
Otherwise, due to autumn season heat (शरद् ऋतु/autumn season) or unwholesome habits, vitiated blood causes conjunctivitis (अभिष्यन्द/conjunctivitis), glaucoma, corneal disease, cataract (तिमिर/cataract), headache, burning sensation, ear diseases, stomatitis (मुखपाक/stomatitis), foul breath, skin tumors, spleen enlargement (प्लीहा/spleen enlargement), deep abscesses (विद्रधि/abscess), erysipelas (विसर्प/erysipelas), fever, bleeding disorders (रक्तपित्त/bleeding disease), skin disorders (कुष्ठ/skin disease), filariasis (श्लीपद/filariasis), gout (वातशोणित/gout), hematuria (रक्तमेह/bloody urine), digestive loss, heaviness, sour belching, salty mouth taste, excessive anger, fainting (मूर्च्छा/fainting), stupor, and tremors.
Diseases that fail to yield to all standard treatments counteracting Vata, Pitta, and Kapha (such as hot, cold, unctuous, or dry therapies) are diagnosed as born of blood aggravation (रक्तप्रकोप/blood vitiation).
Therefore, in such severe blood-borne conditions, the physician should puncture the appropriate vein (सिरा/vein) to drain morbid blood.
However, venesection is contraindicated in persons who have consumed oleation fat, undergone Panchakarma purifications, pregnant women (गर्भिणी/pregnant woman), puerperal mothers (सूतिका/puerperal mother), indigestion sufferers, impissated blood, emaciated children under 16 years, elderly over 70 years, trauma victims, excessive hemorrhage sufferers, un-oleated or un-fomented persons, asthma, severe cough, dyspnea, diarrhea, vomiting, jaundice, anemia (पाण्डु/anemia), ascites, or general body swelling.
Veins that are un-puncturable, improperly bound with tourniquet, un-elevated, oblique, or during extreme cold, heat, wind, or cloudy weather should not be punctured.
In persons who have taken unctuous fats without proper digestion, veins when punctured either fail to flow or bleed excessively.
When a person is properly oleated and fomented, morbid humors detach from tissues, enter the blood stream, and drain out smoothly.
However, these contraindications do not apply in acute emergency poisonings (विष/poison) or acute traumatic conditions.
Selection of specific vein sites for puncturing according to specific diseases:
In head and eye diseases: puncture forehead veins (ललाट्या/forehead vein), nasal bridge veins, or outer eye corner veins.
In ear diseases: puncture veins surrounding the outer ears.
In nasal diseases: puncture the vein at the tip of the nose; in chronic rhinitis, puncture forehead and nasal veins.
In oral diseases: puncture veins situated under the tongue, lips, palate, or jaw.
In head and neck tumors: puncture veins of the neck, ear perimeter, temples, or crown of head.
In epilepsy (अपस्मार/epilepsy): puncture the vein located between jaw joint hinges.
In insanity (उन्माद/insanity): puncture veins of the outer eye corners, chest, or forehead.
In internal abscesses and flank pain: puncture veins of the flanks and armpit boundaries.
In quartan fever (चतुर्थक ज्वर/quartan fever): puncture the vein below the shoulder blade on the affected side.
In tertian fever (तृतीयक ज्वर/tertian fever): puncture the vein located between the shoulders near the sacral trika joint.
In dysentery (प्रवाहिका/dysentery) with severe griping: puncture veins located 2 fingers around the pelvic rim.
In venereal sores (उपदंश/syphilitic sore) and seminal disorders: puncture the central vein of the penis.
In goiter (गलगण्ड/goiter): puncture the vein at the base of the thigh.
In sciatica (गृध्रसी/sciatica): puncture the vein 4 fingers above or below the knee joint.
In cervical lymphadenitis (अपची/lymphadenitis): puncture the vein 2 fingers below the calf muscle Indrabasti marma.
In ankle stiffness and leg Vata pain: puncture the vein 4 fingers above the ankle bone (गुल्फ/ankle).
In elephantiasis (श्लीपद/filariasis): puncture specific veins detailed in its respective chapter.
In foot burning, numbness, corn, gout, and heel fissures: puncture the vein 2 fingers above the Kshipra marma web between the toes.
By these guidelines, corresponding veins of the opposite legs and upper arms are similarly explained.
Specifically, in spleen enlargement (प्लीहोदर/spleen enlargement): puncture the vein in the middle of the left inner arm.
Similarly, in liver enlargement (यकृद्दारुण/hepatomegaly): puncture the vein in the middle of the right inner arm.
The same arm vein puncturing is indicated in severe asthma (श्वास/asthma) and chronic cough (कास/cough).
In upper limb brachial neuralgia (विश्वाची/brachial neuralgia): puncture the arm vein as in sciatica.
Some authorities advise puncturing the upper arm vein in arm wasting and frozen shoulder (अवबाहुक/frozen shoulder).
If these primary veins are invisible, the surgeon may puncture any visible nearby secondary vein in severe diseases.
Before starting, prepare beds, seats, tourniquet bandages, tourniquet rods, and styptic dusting powders.
Keep ready powders of Tagara, Ela, Kushtha, Patha, Vidanga, Devadaru, Trikatu, kitchen soot, and Turmeric mixed with salt and oil to stimulate blood flow if bleeding sluggishly.
Keep ready styptic astringent powders of Lodhra, Madhuka, Priyangu, Gairika, Rasanjana, Shalmali bark, shell ash, barley, wheat, and blackgram flour to arrest bleeding when sufficient.
Also keep ready astringent barks of Banyan, Sacred Fig, Palasha, Bibhitaki, Arjun, and Salla resin for hemostasis.
Also prepare linen ash, lac, cuttlefish bone powder, and emergency resuscitation appliances.
A surgeon equipped with ready surgical accessories never falls into confusion during procedures.
Then, after offering auspicious prayers, the patient (having eaten unctuous meat broth or gruel) is rested, fomented with mild steam/sunlight, and seated facing east on a knees-high soft stool with elbows resting on knees.
Tie a firm cloth or leather headband around the hairline to elevate scalp and facial veins.
The patient holds two firm fists with thumbs tucked inside, pressing against the neck jugulars while puffing out cheeks with clenched teeth.
An assistant standing behind holds the neck band firmly to engorge the target forehead or scalp vein.
When the vein swells prominently like a raised ridge, perform puncturing smoothly without jerking.
For neck, chest, and flank veins: keep the patient sitting upright with arms extended and chest expanded.
For tongue veins: lift the tongue tip upward with a cloth fold, anchoring it against upper teeth before puncturing.
For lip veins: invert the lip with fingers and puncture the inner mucosal vein.
For penis veins: pull the penis downward and puncture the dorsum vein over a firm support.
For arm veins: seat the patient comfortably, massage the arm upward from wrist to elbow, apply a tourniquet 4 fingers above elbow joint, ask the patient to clench fist, and puncture the engorged cubital vein.
For thigh veins: seat the patient, apply a firm tourniquet around upper thigh, and puncture the saphenous vein.
For foot veins: place the foot flat on ground, apply a tourniquet 4 fingers above ankle joint, and puncture the dorsal foot vein.
For ear, nose, or eye perimeter veins: apply local compression and puncture gentle raised veins.
Ensure tourniquets (यन्त्र/tourniquet) are neither too tight nor too loose, allowing venous engorgement without arterial occlusion.
Depth of puncturing: over muscular fleshy regions, puncture to 1 barley grain depth (यव/barley grain) using a paddy-grain lancet (व्रीहिमुख/paddy-grain lancet).
Over thin flesh or skin, puncture to half barley grain depth.
Over bones, puncture to half barley grain depth using an axe lancet (कुठारिका/axe lancet).
In all places, strike the center of the vein smoothly, swiftly, straight, and cleanly without touching underlying vital marma structures (मर्म/vital organ).
Properly punctured vein signs (सम्यक् विद्ध/properly punctured): blood flows out in a continuous steady stream under pressure, and stops smoothly when tourniquet is released.
Improperly punctured signs: if punctured too shallow, blood trickles weakly; if punctured too deep, loud noise occurs and dark venous blood oozes with difficulty.
Causes of poor blood flow despite proper puncture: fainting, fear, loose tourniquet, overly tight tourniquet, extreme hunger, dull blade, or urge suppression.
If blood flow is sluggish, identify the cause and apply warm oil-salt powder rubdown on the vein mouth.
When vitiated blood flows out, it appears dark, thick, or discolored first, like yellow safflower dye washing out.
When pure blood (शुद्ध रक्त/pure blood) begins to flow, it becomes bright red, clear, and stops flowing spontaneously—at that moment, cease bloodletting immediately.
If the patient faints during bloodletting, release tourniquets, sprinkle cold water on face, fan with cool air, speak comforting words, and resume draining after recovery.
If fainting recurs repeatedly, postpone bloodletting to the next day or third day.
Maximum allowable blood loss volume: 1 prastha (प्रस्थ/approx 640 ml) for a strong robust adult.
For weaker individuals, calibrate blood extraction strictly according to disease severity and physical stamina.
Vata-vitiated blood: frothy, thin, reddish-black, dry, non-clotting, tasting astringent, flowing rapidly with cold feel.
Pitta-vitiated blood: yellow, greenish, foul-smelling like raw fish, bitter-pungent, non-clotting with metallic sheen and hot feel.
Kapha-vitiated blood: pale, cold, unctuous, thick, sticky, thread-like, slow-flowing, and salty in taste.
Dual humor vitiated blood shows mixed characteristics of both involved humors.
Tridosha vitiated blood shows dirty blue-black discoloration, turbid consistency, and foul odor.
When bloodletting is complete, compress the vein mouth with thumb, remove tourniquets, clean the site with warm water, apply styptic powder, and bandage with oil-soaked pad.
If bleeding does not stop, dust astringent powders of Lodhra, Gairika, and Sarja resin on the wound and apply firm pressure.
Apply wax-coated pads or sprinkle ice-cold water to induce clot formation (स्कन्दन/clotting).
Give cold, sweet, astringent drinks such as lotus decoctions with sugar and honey, or fresh animal blood fried in ghee for severe blood loss.
If bleeding remains persistent, apply mild alkali (क्षार/alkali) or cauterize the vein mouth with a hot metallic rod (तप्त शलाका/cautery rod).
Never neglect excessive bleeding even for a moment; extreme blood loss aggravates Vata, leading to fainting, convulsions, body tremors, anemia (पाण्डु/anemia), or death.
Blood is the vital life force (प्राण/vital breath) of living beings; when blood is depleted, digestive fire declines and Vata surges—hence nourish the patient immediately with light body-building foods.
If vitiated blood remains residual in tissues, perform a second bloodletting after a fortnight or month after re-oleating the patient.
Post-venesection care: patient must consume warm, light, appetite-kindling, non-spicy foods, avoid physical exertion, anger, daytime sleep, and cold exposure until new blood regenerates.
A person with purified blood possesses clear complexion, sharp senses, robust digestion, sound strength, and joyful mind.
Thus ends the thirty-sixth chapter (षट्त्रिंशः अध्यायः/thirty-sixth chapter) named 'Venesection and Vein Puncturing Rules' (सिराव्यधविधिः/venesection rules) of Ashtanga Sangraha.
Tridosha-vitiated blood appears dark blue, mixed, and foul-smelling.
Pure blood characteristics have been detailed previously.
After blood stops flowing, press the puncture site with thumb along the vein, slowly release the tourniquet, and comfort the patient.
Apply an oil-soaked cloth pad over the vein opening, bind firmly with bandage, and lay the patient down.
If bleeding does not stop, dust the vein opening with styptic astringent powders and press firmly with fingertip.
Apply Shali rice or Upodika mucilage paste over the wound and tie a firm bandage coated with beeswax.
Or sprinkle the site with ice-cold water.
Treat persistent bleeding with cooling, sweet, astringent foods, cold showers, cooling pastes, and wind-protected rooms.
Give sweet milk/sugarcane juice with Padmaka or fresh animal blood (deer, goat, buffalo) fried in ghee.
Or give an unctuous enema processed with grass roots.
Feed the patient with unctuous meat broths and soups.
Or re-puncture the same vein after an interval if blood remains heavily vitiated.
If bleeding completely fails to stop, apply caustic alkali to digest the vessel mouth.
Or cauterize the vein opening with a red-hot metallic rod to constrict the vessel.
Never neglect persistent post-venesection bleeding even for a moment.
For excessive blood loss aggravates Vata in vital organs, causing fainting, unconsciousness, head tremors, dizziness, lockjaw, dyspnea, anemia, deafness, tissue emaciation, convulsions, or death.
Blood is the vital breath of living beings; its depletion destroys digestive fire;
And severely increases Vata humor; therefore, nourish the patient carefully with robust therapies.
Un-cleared vitiated blood should be bled again in the afternoon or on the next day.
After removing remaining morbid blood, pacify the patient with cold showers, cooling pastes, mild purgation, fasting, and sweet unctuous foods.
Or treat with oleation for one month before repeating venesection.
Treat all complications arising from faulty, deep, or slanted vein punctures according to specific medical rules.
Here are the summarizing verses (भवति चात्र/summary verses):
Until displaced humors in blood return to their natural seats following tourniquet pressure;
The patient must strictly observe wholesome diet and lifestyle routines.
After bloodletting, light, easily digestible, appetite-kindling food that is neither too hot nor too cold is wholesome.
For the body with unstable blood must be protected carefully to preserve digestive fire.
A person with clear complexion, bright sense organs, healthy appetite, unobstructed bowel/urinary urges;
Endowed with happiness, nourishment, and physical strength is declared to possess pure blood.
Thus ends the thirty-sixth chapter (षट्त्रिंशः अध्यायः/thirty-sixth chapter) named 'Venesection and Vein Puncturing Rules' (सिराव्यधविधिः/venesection rules) of Ashtanga Sangraha.




Now, therefore, we shall explain the chapter (अध्याय/section) on foreign body extraction techniques (शल्याहरण विधि/foreign body extraction) of Ashtanga Sangraha.
The movement or trajectory of foreign bodies (शल्य/foreign body) is threefold: upward (ऊर्ध्व/upward), downward (अधः/downward), and sideways or oblique (तिर्यक्/oblique).
Each of these three trajectories is further divided into two types: straight (ऋजु/straight) and curved (वक्र/curved).
A wound (व्रण/wound) that is darkish (श्याम/blackish), covered with eruptions (पिडिका/pustules), accompanied by swelling and pain (शोफ वेदना/swelling pain), bleeding repeatedly (शोणितास्राविन्/bleeding), and featuring raised bubble-like soft granulation tissue (मृदु मांस/soft flesh) should be known to contain an embedded foreign body (सशल्य/retained foreign body).
Specifically, when a foreign body lodges in the skin (त्वक्/skin), the swelling is discolored (विवर्ण/discolored), elongated (आयत/elongated), and hard (कठिन/hard).
When it enters muscle tissue (मांस/muscle), there is increasing swelling, non-healing of the foreign body track (शल्य मार्ग/wound track), intolerance to touch or pressure (पीडनासहिष्णुता/pressure tenderness), severe burning sensation (चोष/sucking heat), and suppuration (पाक/suppuration).
When lodged between muscle sheaves (पेशी/muscle sheath), the symptoms resemble those in muscle tissue, except there is no swelling.
When lodged inside a vein (सिरा/vein), there is venous distension (सिराध्मान/vein distension) and sharp pricking pain (शूल/pain).
When lodged in ligaments (स्नायु/ligament), it causes twitching of the ligament network (स्नायुजाल/ligament mesh), severe inflammation (संरम्भ/inflammation), and excruciating pain (उग्र रुक्/severe pain).
When lodged inside body channels (स्रोतो/body channel), it impairs and destroys the natural functions and qualities of those specific channels.
When lodged in an artery (धमनी/artery), Vata forces out frothy blood with a gushing sound (सशब्द/gushing noise), accompanied by body ache and nausea (हृल्लास/nausea).
When lodged inside a bone (अस्थि/bone), it produces various types of severe deep pain and localized swelling (शोफ/swelling).
When lodged in a joint (सन्धि/joint), symptoms resemble those of bone retention along with severe restriction of joint movement (चेष्टोपरोध/movement restriction).
When lodged in the joint crevice of a bone, there is a sensation of bone fullness (अस्थि पूर्णता/bone fullness) and intense grinding crepitus (संघर्ष/grinding friction).
When lodged in abdominal viscera (कोष्ठ/viscera), it causes intestinal gurgling (आटोप/gurgling), abdominal distension (आनाह/distension), and discharge of urine, feces, or food from the wound opening (व्रणमुख/wound mouth).
When lodged in a vital organ (मर्म/vital organ), it produces symptoms of vital organ injury, and the foreign body should be identified by the specific type of discharge (परिस्राव/drainage).
When foreign bodies follow subtle or minute tracks, these identical diagnostic signs manifest in an indistinct or vague manner.
In persons of purified body (शुद्ध देह/purified body), a foreign body aligned with tissue fibers becomes encapsulated and dormant.
However, due to humoral aggravation (दोष प्रकोप/humoral surge) or physical trauma, it moves from its position and causes severe distress again.
When a foreign body is lost under skin (त्वक्/skin), unctuate and foment the skin, rub with mud, blackgram, barley, wheat, and cowdung powder; where inflammation, pain, rapid melting of applied thick ghee (सर्पिस्/ghee), or quick drying of mud paste occurs, the foreign body resides there.
When lost in muscle (मांस/muscle), administer oleation and sudation, then reduce the patient; as muscles loosen and unbind, wherever movement produces inflammation or pain, the foreign body is located there.
Similarly, foreign bodies hidden in abdominal cavities, bone crevices, and muscle spaces should be detected using similar methods.
When lost in veins, channels, arteries, or wound tracks, place the patient on a horse-drawn chariot with broken wheels, drive rapidly over rough roads, and detect its location by emerging inflammation and pain.
When lost in a joint (सन्धि/joint), apply oleation and sudation, then flex, extend, bind, and press the joint to detect its location.
When lost in a bone (अस्थि/bone), apply oleation and sudation, then firmly bind and press the bone to locate it.
When lost in a vital organ (मर्म/vital organ), testing is conducted through muscle, blood, and bone examinations since vital organs are composed of these same tissues.
General detection rule: riding elephants, horses, or trees, fast driving, jumping, swimming, exercise, yawning, belching, coughing, sneezing, spitting, laughing, breath control, or passing stool, urine, or semen—wherever pain, swelling, numbness, heaviness, or distress arises upon slight exertion, a foreign body is embedded there.
In brief, foreign bodies (शल्य/foreign body) are fourfold based on shape: hand-like (हस्त/hand-shaped), two-angled (द्विकोण/two-angled), three-angled (त्रिकोण/three-angled), or four-angled (चतुष्कोण/four-angled).
When invisible inside tissue, its precise shape should be inferred from the outer shape and orientation of the wound (व्रण/wound).
For extracting large foreign bodies, there are only two main directional methods: counter-directional extraction (प्रतिलोम/against direction) and along-directional extraction (अनुलोम/along direction).
Counter-directional extraction pulls it backward if the entry face points downward or toward the entry wound; along-directional pushes or pulls it forward if it points through to the opposite side.
If lodged transversely or obliquely (तिर्यक्/oblique), cut open the overlying tissue at whichever side allows easiest extraction and remove it.
Do not shake or forcefully pull out a barbed or hooked foreign body counter-directionally if its tip requires cutting; likewise, do not extract foreign bodies lodged in armpits, groins, or between ribs.
Do not extract a foreign body if it has entered a fatal vital organ (विशल्यघ्न मर्म/fatal upon extraction) or lies deeply hidden without swelling, pain, or suppuration.
Now, a foreign body accessible directly to fingers should be extracted using bare hands (हस्त/hand).
If hand extraction is impossible, extract it using appropriate surgical forceps or instruments (यन्त्र/instrument).
If forceps fail, cut open tissue with a scalpel (शस्त्र/scalpel), clear blood from wound, foment with warm ghee, cauterize, soothe with honey and ghee (सर्पिस् मधु/ghee honey), bandage, and prescribe post-operative care.
If entangled in veins or ligaments, dislodge it gently using the tip of a metallic probe (शलाका/probe) before extracting.
If stuck near the heart, startle the patient with cold water sprays to alter cardiac spasm and extract along its entry track.
If bound tightly by surrounding tissue, cut open the constricting tissue and pull it out.
If wedged inside a bone crevice or bitten tight by bone, brace the patient with both feet and pull out the foreign body with forceps.
If still stuck, hold the patient firmly with strong assistants, grip the foreign body shaft with forceps or bowstring, and tie the other end to the bit ring of a five-point tethered horse.
Then whip the horse so that as it jerks its head upward with sudden force, it pulls out the foreign body.
Alternatively, bend down a springy strong tree branch, tie the foreign body cord to it, and release the branch so its upward recoil extracts it.
If the shaft or handle is weak, bind it securely with strong leather thongs or cords before pulling.
If the foreign body shaft is swallowed by tissue swelling, squeeze and press back the swelling first to expose the shaft.
If an unbarbed foreign body is jammed in bone, loosen it by gentle tapping with a stone mallet (अश्ममुद्गर/stone mallet) and extract along its track.
If barbed with side ears, crush or flatten the ears using tubular forceps (नाडीयन्त्र/tubular speculum) or probes before extracting.
An unbarbed iron foreign body aligned along tissue fibers in an open wound should be extracted using a magnet (अयस्कान्त/magnet).
A foreign body lodged in the large intestine or rectum should be expelled using purgative therapy (विरेचन/purgation).
Obstruction of Vata, feces, urine, or retained fetus should be relieved by bearing down straining (प्रवाहण/bearing down).
Trapped morbid air, poison, or bad milk should be extracted by suction using animal horns (शृङ्ग/horn), gourds, or suction pots.
If a foreign body lodges in the throat passage (कण्ठ/throat), pass a string attached to a lotus-stalk fiber down the throat.
Once the foreign body entangles in the lotus fibers, pull out the string and lotus stalk together.
In the absence of lotus-stalk fibers, follow the same method using tender lotus roots (मृणाल/lotus root).
If a lac or wax object lodges in the throat, insert a tube down the throat, then pass a fine-tipped heated metallic probe through it.
When the heated probe melts into the lac object, cool it with cold water sprays so it solidifies onto the probe, then pull it out.
For non-lac objects in the throat, insert a probe coated with melted hot lac or beeswax (मधूच्छिष्ट/beeswax), let it stick, cool, and extract.
If a fish bone or sharp bone stuck in throat, remove using a finger wrapped in thread or cloth; or insert a thread-bound mop.
Alternatively, swallow a hair-ball (केशाण्डुक/hair ball) tied to a strong long thread with fluid, then induce vomiting (वमन/emesis).
As the patient vomits and the bone catches the hair-ball, jerk the string out suddenly to pull the bone with it.
Or push it down or dislodge it using a soft herbal toothbrush mop (दन्तधावनकूर्च/herbal brush mop).
Or push it down into the stomach while bowing the neck forward.
Similarly, treat a sharp thorn or bone caught in a ball of hair.
If the throat is scratched or injured, lick Triphala powder mixed with honey, ghee, and sugar candy.
For a drowned person filled with water, hold upside down by feet, compress chest, shake, induce vomiting, or bury up to face in warm ash pile (भस्म/ash heap).
Otherwise, trapped water entering wrong channels causes abdominal distension, cough, asthma, rhinitis, sensory loss, fever, and death.
In such cases, treat each resulting complication according to its specific therapeutic rules.
If a food bolus (ग्रास शल्य/food bolus) chokes the throat, swallow water or strike the shoulder firmly with a fist.
Trapped throat phlegm or soft lump should be dislodged by blowing powder (प्रधमन/blowing powder), coughing, or throat clearing.
A minute foreign body in the eye should be removed using scraping drops, powder blowing, hair, water, cloth edge, or tongue.
Evert the eyelid, remove the foreign body, apply warm steam fomentation, and wash with liquorice decoction, honey, and ghee.
Foreign bodies are often expelled naturally from eyes and body organs through tears, sneezing (क्षवथु/sneezing), coughing, belching, urine, feces, and flatus.
When an insect enters the ear canal, it produces pricking pain, heaviness, buzzing noise, and severe pain whenever it moves.
Instill warm salt water, honey-vinegar, or wine into the ear canal to kill or float out the insect.
Once the insect floats out or dies, drain the liquid from the ear.
If a dead insect remains inside, it causes ear suppuration (पाक/suppuration), putrefaction (कोथ/putrefaction), and foul discharge.
In such cases, administer therapies prescribed for ear discharge (कर्णस्राव/ear discharge) and ear blockage.
If water enters the ear, fill with warm oil-water mix, tilt the head sideways, slap the opposite side, or suction out.
Here are the summary verses:
Foreign bodies made of lac (जातुष/lac) or metals like gold and silver dissolve slowly over time due to bodily internal heat (ऊष्मन्/body heat).
Indeed, metallic and resinous foreign bodies often soften or melt inside tissue from endogenous body heat.
However, foreign bodies made of horn, bamboo, wood, bone, teeth, hair, stone, and clay do not dissolve inside the body.
Such organic and mineral foreign bodies remain intact and cause localized tissue destruction.
Splinters of horn, bamboo, or palm wood cause rapid tissue suppuration and muscle-blood rotting even after long retention.
They split tissue fibers, cause intense inflammation, and quickly suppurate surrounding flesh and blood.
If a foreign body is deeply embedded in muscle and does not burst open naturally:
Subject the area to massage, sudation, purificatory evacuations, reducing diet, or nourishing diet as needed;
Apply sharp poultices, internal medications, and deep scalpel incisions (शस्त्र पद/scalpel incision) to ripen the abscess;
Once suppurated, extract the foreign body through incision (पाटन/incision), probing (एषण/probing), and squeezing (पीडन/squeezing).
Considering the diverse shapes of foreign bodies, body regions, and surgical instruments:
An intelligent surgeon should carefully locate and extract foreign bodies using appropriate clever techniques.
When a wound becomes clean, smooth at margins, free from severe tenderness, swelling, and heat:
With minimal swelling and fever, it should be declared completely free of foreign bodies (निःशल्य/free of foreign body).
The physical body itself becomes the greatest foreign body when clogged with morbid internal humors and metabolic wastes.
External foreign bodies like arrows (शर/arrow) are secondary compared to the severe internal affliction caused by morbid humors.
Thus ends the thirty-seventh chapter (सप्तत्रिंशः अध्यायः/thirty-seventh chapter) named 'Foreign Body Extraction Techniques' (शल्याहरणविधिः/foreign body extraction rules) of Ashtanga Sangraha.




Now, therefore, we shall explain the chapter (अध्याय/section) on eightfold surgical operations and wound care rules (शस्त्रकर्म विधि/surgical procedures) of Ashtanga Sangraha.
In both types of diseases requiring surgical intervention—whether internal organic (निज/internal) or traumatic exogenous (आगन्तु/exogenous)—surgical operation is applied when conditions exceed the scope of internal medicine (भेषज/internal medicine).
Such surgical ailments are usually preceded by inflammatory swelling (शोफ/inflammatory swelling).
Therefore, while the swelling is in its early un-suppurated stage, recognizing whether it is driven by Vata, Pitta, Kapha, Blood, or combined humors, and to prevent suppuration, one should apply fasting (उपवास/fasting), herbal pastes (लेप/paste), fomentation showers, bloodletting (असृङमोक्ष/bloodletting), astringent decoctions, ghee drinking, and evacuative purifications.
If it still does not subside, liquefying resolvent therapy (प्रविलयन/dissolving therapy) should be applied.
If it continues to throb and increase, warm poultices (उपनाहन/warm poultice) should be applied.
In that context, the unripe stage (आम लक्षण/unripe stage) of swelling is characterized by slight swelling, low heat, mild pain, skin retaining its natural color (त्वक्सवर्णता/natural skin color), and firm stability.
The suppurating stage (पच्यमान/suppurating) is discolored, stretched like a blown bladder, with severe throbbing, sharp burning (दाह/burning heat), thirst, fever, restlessness, intolerance to touch, insomnia, oozing of liquid, drying up of applied pastes, prickling like mustard paste, crawling ant sensations, feelings of being beaten with sticks, pricked with needles, pierced with spears, cut with scalpels, bitten by scorpions, or burnt with fire and alkali.
The fully suppurated/ripe stage (पक्व/ripe) is characterized by subsidence of sharp throbbing, local flaccidity, skin slackness and wrinkling, paleness, central elevation, pitting rebound upon finger pressure, fluctuation of pus (पूय संचरण/pus fluctuation) like water in a bladder, and subsidence of itching and pain.
Pain does not occur without Vata; burning does not occur without Pitta; swelling does not occur without Kapha.
Redness occurs due to Blood, and suppuration occurs due to Pitta combined with vitiated blood.
If an abscess is left un-incised after full suppuration, it forms deep hollow tracts, thins the overlying skin, and eats away tissue.
The skin becomes wrinkled, darkish-grey, and hair falls off.
In certain Kapha-dominant swellings, due to deep location, blood undergoes internal suppuration deep within.
In such cases, classic signs of external suppuration remain obscure.
Where skin retains normal color, local coldness exists, pain is minimal, and hardness is stone-like, the surgeon should not be confused; this is called deep blood suppuration (रक्तपाक/deep blood suppuration).
Having confirmed full suppuration, if the patient is timid, elderly, child, weak, delicate, pregnant, or if the swelling is small, located on joints or vital spots, chemical bursting (दारण/chemical bursting) using sharp hot paste should be applied.
In all other suitable cases, surgical incision (पाटन/incision) should be performed.
Incising an unripe swelling causes severe injury to veins and ligaments, excessive hemorrhaging, excruciating pain, tissue tearing, or traumatic erysipelas.
Conversely, allowing trapped pus to remain inside burns deep veins, ligaments, blood, and flesh rapidly like fire burning dry grass.
He who incises an unripe swelling out of ignorance, and he who neglects a fully suppurated swelling out of hesitation—
Both are to be known as incompetent practitioners acting without clinical certainty.
Prior to surgery, feed the patient wholesome sweet food and administer alcoholic drinks (except in obstructed labor, ascites, urinary stones, and mouth disorders) so they do not faint and feel less surgical pain.
Prior to surgery, feed the patient wholesome sweet food and administer alcoholic drinks.
Due to food in the stomach, they do not faint; due to mild intoxication, they do not feel the blade.
Except in cases of obstructed labor, ascites, bladder stones, and oral diseases.
The surgeon, equipped with all blunt instruments, scalpels, alkali, cautery, sponges, threads, bandages, and dressings, seating the patient facing east and facing west himself, avoiding vital spots, veins, and ligaments, shall make a swift, clean incision along hair directions until pus appears.
Even in large suppurated abscesses, the scalpel entry length is stated as two fingers.
Probing the sinus depth with a probe (एषणी/probe) or lotus stalk to locate hidden pus cavities.
Wherever sinus tracks or pus pockets extend, the surgeon should open them with well-separated incisions to leave no un-drained cavity.
Making long and broad incisions so that no morbid pus remains trapped.
Surgical excellence requires courage (शौर्य/courage), swift execution (आशुक्रिया/swift action), sharp instruments, steady un-sweating hands, and presence of mind (असंमोह/presence of mind).
Courage, swift execution, sharp scalpel, un-sweating steady hands, and presence of mind are lauded in surgical operations.
Transverse incisions (तिर्यक् छेद/transverse incision) are specifically prescribed on eyebrows, cheeks, forehead, eyelids, lips, gums, neck, collarbone, armpit, abdomen, and groin.
Transverse incisions are directed across eyebrows, cheeks, forehead, eyelids, lips, gums, neck, collarbones, armpits, abdomen, and groins.
On other body parts, transverse cuts sever veins and ligaments, cause extreme pain, delayed healing, and fleshy growths.
After incision, soothing the patient with cold water, expressing pus, washing with astringent decoction, fumigating with Guggulu and neem leaves, applying medicated wick (वर्ती/wick), covering with barley flour poultice, and applying a firm bandage.
Bandaging Vata-Kapha wounds two or three times; Pitta-blood wounds once.
Bandaging Vata-Kapha wounds two or three times; Pitta-blood wounds once.
Pouring warm Liquorice-ghee decoction over painful surgical wounds.
Sprinkling water from a water pot and performing protective rituals (रक्षा विधान/protective ritual) using Rakshoghna herbs to guard against evil spirits.
Offering sacrifices and prayers for healing.
Removing the bandage on the third day, washing the wound, and re-applying dressings.
Unbinding on the second day in summer/autumn; on the third day in winter/spring.
Changing dressings incorrectly causes severe pain, suppuration, and wound deterioration.
Applying astringent, sweet, and soothing medicated ghee to heal the wound.
Eightfold surgical operations (अष्टविध शस्त्रकर्म/eightfold surgical acts): Excision (छेदन), Slitting (भेदन), Scraping (लेखन), Puncturing (व्यधन), Probing (एषण), Extraction (आहरण), Drainage (विस्रावण), and Suturing (सीवन).
Excision for deep lumps and growths; Slitting for suppurated abscesses; Scraping for hyperpigmentation and slough; Puncturing for veins and fluid sacs; Probing for sinuses; Extraction for foreign bodies and stones; Drainage for blood and pus; Suturing for clean fresh wounds.
Suturing (सीवन/suturing) is indicated in clean, un-infected wounds, fresh accidental wounds, excised earlobes, and suturable visceral or skin incisions.
Three types of suturing needles (सूची/needle): round 2-finger needle for joints and thin skin; 3-finger triangular-tipped needle for thick fleshy parts; 1.5-finger bow-curved needle for internal organs and viscera.
Needles must be well-made with hidden eyes and strong threads.
Using threads of hemp, silk, sinew, or bark fibers.
Four suturing stitches (सीवन विकल्प/suturing stitch): Goshphanika (sling pattern), Tunnaseevani (tailor seam), Vellitaka (spiral pattern), and Granthibandhana (knotted pattern).
Stitch selection based on wound shape and anatomical location.
Avoiding placing stitches too close together or too far apart.
Dusting sutured wounds with astringent healing powders (Lodhra, Priyangu, Sallaki, Licorice) mixed with honey and ghee, then applying dressings.
Wounds caused by poison, fire, alkali, or retaining internal air or foreign bodies should NOT be sutured.
Wounds with low flesh or over moving joints should be sutured carefully.
Fifteen specialized surgical bandages (पञ्चदश बन्ध/15 bandage types): Kosha, Dama, Uttanga, Swastika, Anuvestita, Muttoli, Mandala, Sthagika, Yamaka, Khatva, China, Vibandha, Vitana, Gophana, Panchangi.
Application of Kosha on fingers/joints; Dama on tight limbs; Uttanga on hanging parts; Swastika on joints, groin, armpits, cheeks, ears; Anuvestita on limbs; Muttoli on neck/penis; Mandala on round parts; Sthagika on fingertips/stumps; Yamaka on twin wounds; Khatva on jaw/cheeks; China on outer eye corners; Vibandha on back/abdomen; Vitana on head; Gophana on nose/chin/scrotum; Panchangi on parts above clavicle.
Bandages placed securely without placing knots directly over wound orifices.
Three levels of bandaging pressure: Tight (गाढ/tight), Medium (सम/medium), and Loose (शिथिल/loose).
Tight pressure on buttocks, armpits, groins, thighs, and head; Medium pressure on limbs, face, ears, neck, penis, scrotum, back, sides, and abdomen; Loose pressure over eyes and joints.
Adjusting bandaging tightness according to season and humoral dominance.
Re-bandaging every 3 days in winter/spring; morning and evening in summer/autumn.
Unbandaged wounds become exposed to flies, dust, cold, wind, and trauma, causing severe complications, delayed healing, and abnormal scarring.
Unbandaged wounds suffer from medicine drying quickly, slow healing, and permanent skin discoloration.
Crushed, fractured, or severely torn bones, ligaments, and veins do not heal without proper bandaging support.
Properly bandaged wounds allow the patient to sleep and move without pain.
Features of an unhealthy, improperly healed wound: raised edges, uneven, hard, and painful.
Features of a clean, properly healed wound: level with surrounding skin, soft, painless, and quickly healed.
Covering dry, low-flesh, non-healing wounds with fresh tree leaves (पत्र प्रदेह/leaf cover) matching humor and season.
Leaves of Kshiri trees, Bhurja, Arjuna, and Kadamba used for leaf dressings.
Bandaging prohibited in leprosy, diabetic carbuncles, alkali burns, poison burns, severe anal inflammation, and spreading erysipelas.
Bandaging prohibited in severe tissue suppuration, anal sloughing, spreading erysipelas, and burning swelling.
If flies lay eggs in an exposed wound, maggots (कृमि/maggot) develop, devouring tissue and causing severe pain and bleeding.
Maggot-infested wounds treated with Surasadi herbal wash, cow urine paste, and alkali water pouring.
Covering maggot wounds with fresh meat slices to draw out maggots rapidly.
Treating evolving wound conditions according to humoral changes without rushing to close a wound that retains internal morbidity.
Prematurely closed wounds with trapped morbidity form deep internal sinus tracks and break open again.
Therefore, only a completely cleansed, purified wound should be allowed to heal.
Even after complete healing, the patient must avoid indigestion, physical exertion, and sexual intercourse for 6 to 7 months.
This general directional guide for wound treatment is stated here; detailed therapeutics will be elaborated in Uttara-tantra.
Thus ends the thirty-eighth chapter (अष्टात्रिंशः अध्यायः/thirty-eighth chapter) named 'Eightfold Surgical Operations and Wound Care Rules' (शस्त्रकर्मविधिः/surgical procedures) of Ashtanga Sangraha.
Application of Kosha bandage on finger joints.
Dama bandage on narrow or tight limb parts.
Uttanga bandage on hanging or drooping structures.
Swastika cross bandage on joints, inter-mammary space, armpits, cheeks, and ears.
Anuvestita spiral bandage around limbs.
Muttoli circular wrap on neck and penis.
Mandala circular bandage on round body parts.
Sthagika thimble bandage on thumb, fingers, and penile tip.
Yamaka double bandage for twin adjacent wounds.
Khatva four-tailed bandage for jaw, temples, and cheeks.
China bandage for outer corners of eyes.
Vibandha binder bandage for abdomen, chest, and back.
Vitana canopy bandage for head and broad body surfaces.
Gophana sling bandage for nose, lips, chin, and pelvis.
Panchangi five-tailed bandage for parts above the collarbone.
Bandages should be applied appropriately to each region without placing knots directly over the wound.
Bandaging is desired in Vata disorders, severe fractures, and deep traumatic wounds.
Bandaging is applied clockwise or counter-clockwise according to the side of the body.
Pressure is classified into three levels: tight (गाढ/tight), medium (सम/medium), and loose (शिथिल/loose).
Tight bandaging for Kapha-Vata; loose bandaging for Pitta-Blood.
Medium bandaging for Vata-Pitta and Kapha-Pitta wounds.
Tight bandaging is applied over buttocks, armpits, groins, thighs, and head.
Medium bandaging over limbs, face, ears, neck, penis, scrotum, back, sides, and abdomen.
Loose bandaging over eyes and joint junctions.
In Vata-Kapha wounds: apply medium pressure on loose sites, tight on medium sites, and tighter on tight sites.
Re-bandage every 3 days in winter and spring; change daily in summer and autumn.
In Pitta-Blood wounds: apply loose pressure on medium sites, and re-bandage morning and evening in autumn and summer.
Unbandaged wounds exposed to mosquitoes, dust, cold, and wind suffer severe complications and turn septic.
Ointments dry up quickly on unbandaged wounds, leading to slow healing and discolored scars.
Furthermore:
Crushed, fractured, or severed bones, ligaments, and veins do not heal without firm bandaging support.
Firm bandaging stabilizes severed bones, ligaments, and blood vessels to enable complete tissue regeneration.
Properly bandaged patients do not experience pain during standing, sitting, or sleeping.
Unhealthy, improperly healed wounds have raised edges, uneven contours, hardness, and continuous pain.
A clean, healthy wound is level with surrounding skin, soft, painless, and heals rapidly.
Dry, low-flesh, non-healing wounds should be covered with fresh leaves (पत्र प्रदेह/leaf cover) appropriate to humor and season.
Covering the medicated wound with fresh tree leaves matching the season and humor.
Using smooth, clean leaves perforated with small holes to allow aeration.
Leaves of Kshiri trees, Bhurja, Arjuna, and Kadamba are used for leaf dressings.
Bandaging is prohibited in leprosy, diabetic carbuncles, alkali burns, and poison burns.
Bandaging is prohibited in rat-bite poison, alkali burns, and toxic ulcers.
Bandaging is contraindicated in severe tissue suppuration and severe anal inflammation.
In sloughing, burning, swelling, or spreading erysipelas, bandaging is avoided.
In neglected exposed wounds, flies lay eggs that hatch into maggots (कृमि/maggot).
Maggots devour surrounding tissue, causing intense pain, swelling, and blood discharge.
Maggot-infested wounds should be washed and filled with Surasadi herbal decoction.
Applying paste of Saptaparna, Karanja, Arka, Neem, and Rajadana bark processed in cow urine, washed with alkali water.
Covering the wound with a fresh piece of meat draws out maggots rapidly.
Covering with fresh meat entices maggots out so they can be removed quickly.
Evolving wound stages must be managed according to humoral changes without rushing to close a wound with trapped morbidity.
A prematurely closed wound with trapped internal pus forms deep sinus tracks and re-opens with greater severity.
Therefore, only a thoroughly purified, clean wound should be allowed to heal.
Even after complete healing, the patient must avoid indigestion, physical exertion, and sexual intercourse for 6 to 7 months.
This protective post-healing regimen for 6 to 7 months ensures permanent recovery.
This concise guide covers the fundamental principles of wound management.
Detailed therapeutic management of individual wounds will be elaborated in Uttara-tantra.
Thus ends the thirty-eighth chapter (अष्टात्रिंशः अध्यायः/thirty-eighth chapter) named 'Eightfold Surgical Operations and Wound Care Rules' (शस्त्रकर्मविधिः/surgical procedures) of Ashtanga Sangraha.




Now, therefore, we shall explain the chapter (अध्याय/section) on chemical cautery and alkali application rules (क्षारकर्म विधि/alkali cautery rules) of Ashtanga Sangraha.
Alkali (क्षार/alkali) is supreme among surgical and auxiliary instruments because it performs excision (छेदन/excision), slitting (भेदन/slitting), scraping (लेखन/scraping), clearing, drying, and healing due to its sharp, hot, burning, and liquefying properties, counteracting all humors, fat, and poison.
Alkali application is twofold: external application (बहिः परिमार्जन/external application) and internal administration (अन्तः परिमार्जन/internal potion).
External application is used for hemorrhoids (अर्शस्/piles), tumors (अर्बुद/tumor), fistula-in-ano (भगन्दर/fistula), sinus tracts, skin keloids, mole marks, facial skin diseases, leprosy, and ringworm.
Internal administration is used for abdominal tumors (गुल्म/phantom tumor), internal poisoning (गर/poisoning), ascites (उदर/dropsy), indigestion, severe colic, intestinal obstruction, and urinary calculus (अश्मरी/stone).
Internal alkali potions will be detailed in their respective disease chapters.
Alkali is contraindicated in timid, weak, emaciated persons, acute fever, diarrhea, jaundice, bleeding disorders, eye inflammation, pregnancy, infant/elderly, and generalized body pain.
Alkali must not be applied directly over vital spots (मर्म/vital spot), blood vessels (सिरा/blood vessel), ligaments (स्नायु/ligament), joints, cartilage, testicles, navel, nails, or thin-fleshed parts, nor on eyes (except eyelid diseases).
Alkali should not be applied during extreme cold, intense heat, rain, cloud cover, or strong winds.
External alkali is prepared in three strengths: mild (मृदु/mild strength), medium (मध्यम/medium strength), and sharp/intense (तीक्ष्ण/intense strength).
Method of alkali preparation: In autumn, on an auspicious clean day, the physician worshipping an un-injured middle-aged Mushkaka (Kalamushkaka) tree with offerings...
Offering prayer to the tree deities: 'Salutations to the deities residing here; please depart peacefully...'
'I am gathering this tree for the therapeutic benefit of all living beings.'
'I take this plant not out of greed or anger, but for medicine.'
Thus praying to the tree before cutting.
On the next day, if no ill omens appear, the physician cuts the tree facing east or north.
Felling the tree at sunrise after chanting auspicious mantras.
Burning pieces of Mushkaka along with Paribhadra, Palasha, Aragvadha, Tilvaka, Kutaja, Chitraka, Arka, Apamarga, and limestone rocks using sesamum stalks in a windless pit.
After cooling, separating the limestone ash, dissolving 1 drona of alkali ash in 1,000 palas of water or cow urine, straining through a thick cloth 21 times until a clear, reddish, sharp, slimy alkali lye liquid (क्षार निष्यन्द/alkali lye liquid) is obtained.
Then boiling the alkali lye liquid over fire according to fat-boiling rules.
During boiling, adding fine powder of burnt lime, conch shell, and oyster shell as a strengthening agent (प्रतिवाप/admixture).
Stirring continuously with a large wooden ladle.
When the liquid becomes thick, bubbling, and coats the ladle, removing from fire and storing in a sealed iron jar buried in a heap of barley.
This constitutes the Medium Alkali (मध्यम क्षार/medium alkali).
Mild Alkali (मृदु क्षार/mild alkali) is prepared without adding roasted shell powders.
Sharp/Intense Alkali (तीक्ष्ण क्षार/sharp alkali) is prepared by adding fine powders of Danti, Chitraka, Langali, Hingu, Vacha, Trivrit, Bida salt, Sarjika, and Kanakakshiri.
Storing for 7 days before therapeutic application according to disease strength.
If the alkali lye loses potency, adding fresh alkali liquid to recharge its strength.
Ten ideal qualities of alkali (दश क्षार गुण/10 alkali virtues): neither too sharp nor too mild, white, smooth, fast-acting, slimy, non-spreading, easily neutralized, causing minimal pain, and clear.
Ten faults of improperly prepared alkali (दश क्षार दोष/10 alkali defects):
Namely:
Too hot, too cold, too sharp, too mild, too thin, too thick, excessively slimy, spreading uncontrollably, deficient in herbs, or under-cooked.
Equipment needed for alkali cautery: cotton swabs, probes, ladles, leeches, ghee, honey, sour gruel, whey, milk, cold water, and soft beds.
Procedure: Seating the patient securely, scraping or incising the diseased lesion, applying alkali using a probe or cotton swab.
Observing the lesion for 100 time counts (मात्रा/time count) after applying alkali.
In eyelid diseases, everting the eyelid, protecting the cornea with a beeswax pad, and applying a lotus-leaf-thin coat of alkali.
In nasal hemorrhoids/polyp, elevating the nostril while the patient faces the sun, applying alkali for 50 time counts.
Similarly applying alkali in ear polyps/tumors.
In rectal piles (गुदार्शस्/rectal piles), inserting a slotted proctoscope, applying alkali through the slot, and holding for 100 time counts.
Wiping off the alkali, inspecting for proper cauterization, and neutralizing (निर्वापण/neutralizing wash) immediately with medicated ghee, honey, sour gruel, or milk.
Applying cooling sweet pastes mixed with ghee over the neutralized site.
Feeding the patient moisture-increasing food to promote sloughing.
If the cauterized eschar does not slough off due to deep roots, applying sesame paste with sour gruel, licorice, Trivrit, and Sariva.
Washing the resulting wound with a decoction of Malati, Neem, Patola, and Karaveera leaves.
Healing the wound with medicated ghee or oil boiled with Nagakeshara, Manjistha, Sandalwood, and Sesame leaves.
Treating the wound according to humoral imbalance.
